**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 2 ("Then, the next moment, I saw Miss Mia run to the left!") and Testimony 4 ("She turned, and ran for the door!"). White states Mia ran to the left, then ran for the door. However, Testimony 3 states "She turned, and ran for the door!" implying a single, continuous action. **Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

Prop 3: Mia ran to the left after dodging the attack.
Prop 4: The door is to the right of Mia when she ran.
Prop 5: The killer is near to Mia when she ran to the door.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

Propositions 3 and 4 are directly relevant. Prop 3 states Mia ran to the *left*. Prop 4 states the door is to Mia's *right* when she ran. These two propositions are incompatible. Testimony 2 reinforces Mia running to the left, directly contradicting the spatial relationship established by Prop 4 and implied by Testimony 4. Testimony 3 further complicates the picture by suggesting a single, continuous movement towards the door.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

The spatial reasoning is based on the directional terms "left" and "right." If Mia ran to the left, the door cannot simultaneously be to her right. The testimonies provide conflicting spatial information about Mia's movement, creating a contradiction.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

The spatial reasoning is straightforward: a location can only be one direction relative to another. The victim cannot simultaneously run to the left and the right. The contradiction arises from the conflicting spatial descriptions of the victim's initial movement.

**Step 3:** **If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

**Step 4:** **If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

**Step 5:** **Provide your answer in JSON format**

## CRITICAL OUTPUT REQUIREMENT:
**Your answer must end with a JSON format like so:** {"evidence": 2, "testimony": 3}

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, with nothing before or after it.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Evidence 3 (Letter) and Testimony 4. Testimony 4 states that Ms. Crane "tucked safely away" the letter in her "left breast pocket." However, Evidence 3 (Letter) states that the letter was "inside Ms. Crane's left breast pocket." While seemingly similar, the phrasing "tucked safely away" implies an action *performed by Ms. Crane*, suggesting she was alive and capable of placing the letter there. Testimony 3 states Ms. Crane "managed to retaliate" before dying, implying a final act. However, if she was already dying, it's questionable whether she could have deliberately and safely tucked the letter into her pocket. The implication is that someone else placed the letter there *after* her death.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 3: The letter from Kay was inside Ms. Crane's left breast pocket.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 3 directly relates to the contradiction. The proposition states the letter *was* in Ms. Crane's pocket. Testimony 4 implies Ms. Crane placed it there. However, the context of her dying state, as described in Testimony 3, casts doubt on her ability to perform this action. Prop 3 becomes problematic because it doesn't account for the possibility that someone else placed the letter there post-mortem. The spatial location (left breast pocket) is confirmed by Prop 3, but the *circumstances* of it being there are called into question by the combined testimonies.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning**

N/A - Prop 3 directly applies.

The contradiction arises because Ini's testimonies suggest Mimi was extremely particular about who drove her car, yet Prop 5 places Ini in the passenger seat, implying Mimi was driving. This spatial arrangement, combined with Mimi's supposed protectiveness of her car, creates the contradiction.

**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The presence of the urn suggests a level of control and awareness that contradicts his testimony of being incapacitated.

Prop 1: The Sacred Urn was inside the Urn Box at the time of the crime.

The fact that the urn *was* in his office (Prop 2) contradicts his claim of being incapacitated during a struggle (Testimony 0-5). Prop 1 and Prop 10 are relevant because they describe the urn's container and its condition, further emphasizing the implausibility of Atmey's story. If he was attacked, the urn would likely have been disturbed or moved, not neatly placed in his office.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning is based on the physical impossibility of being simultaneously incapacitated and responsible for the secure placement of an object. The location of the Sacred Urn (Evidence 4) creates a spatial conflict with the narrative of a violent attack (Testimony 0-5).

------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 1 ("That woman, she came back down on the elevator alone.") and Testimony 2 ("After that, there was no sign of the president getting on the elevator at all!"). These testimonies directly contradict each other regarding who used the elevator to descend from the roof of the Bigg Building. One states Judge Gavèlle used the elevator alone, while the other states the President did not use the elevator at all.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: President Wang was inside the Bigg Building at the same time as Judge Gavèlle. This proposition is consistent with the initial events described in the testimonies.
Prop 2: The elevator on the roof of the Bigg Building was disconnected from the lower floors at the time of the incident. This proposition *directly* contradicts both testimonies. If the elevator was disconnected, neither Gavèlle nor Wang could have used it.
Prop 10: The pocket chess set was inside the auction storeroom at the time of Bronco Knight's murder. This is irrelevant to the contradiction.

**Step 3: Propositions revealing the contradiction**

Prop 2 directly reveals the contradiction. If the elevator was disconnected, the events described in testimonies 1 and 2 are impossible. Either the testimonies are false, or the elevator was not disconnected as claimed.

**Step 4: Spatial reasoning**

The core spatial reasoning is about the elevator's functionality and its connection to the lower floors. The testimonies describe a sequence of events involving the elevator. If the elevator was disconnected, the sequence is impossible, creating a spatial contradiction.

However, she claims to have seen *no one* on the way. This is contradictory because if she was actively escorting someone, she would have to be in the same space as that person.

Prop 2: The Channeling Chamber is north of the Meditation Room.

Prop 1: The Side Room is to the east of the Meditation Room.

These propositions establish a spatial relationship between the Side Room, Meditation Room, and Channeling Chamber. Ini's testimony implies a journey between these locations.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 3, "The winding path connects the Meditation Room and the Side Room," is particularly relevant. The story states Ini walked her sister to the Channeling Chamber *from the Side Room*. This means she would have had to traverse the winding path, passing through the Meditation Room. If she was escorting her sister, they would have been in the same space, and it's highly improbable she wouldn't have seen her sister. Her claim of seeing no one contradicts the act of escorting someone through a defined path.

**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Crime Photo 2 (Evidence 3) and Testimony 3. Crime Photo 2 states the victim's watch stopped at 3:05. This creates a spatial and temporal contradiction: if the fall happened before 3:05, and the autopsy states death at 3:00 PM, there's a discrepancy in the timing of the fall relative to the time of death. The umbrella being involved in the fall suggests a physical proximity and interaction that needs to be reconciled with the established time of death.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

*   **Prop 4:** Phoenix was near the umbrella when the victim fell on it. This directly relates to the fall described in Testimony 3.
*   **Prop 5:** The victim was lying head down behind the Ivy U. building. This establishes the location of the body after the fall.
*   **Prop 10:** Phoenix was facing the victim when he fell on the umbrella. This further clarifies the spatial relationship between Phoenix and the victim during the fall.

**Step 3: Propositions revealing the contradiction**

Propositions 4, 5, and 10 highlight the contradiction. Testimony 3 places Phoenix near the umbrella and describes the victim falling *onto* it. Prop 5 confirms the victim's final location – lying behind the Ivy U. building. Prop 10 specifies Phoenix's orientation towards the victim during the fall. However, the autopsy report (Evidence 1) states the time of death was 3:00 PM, while the watch stopped at 3:05. This suggests a significant time gap between the fall (as described in Testimony 3) and the actual time of death, which is inconsistent with a single, immediate fatal event. The spatial context provided by Props 4, 5, and 10 emphasizes the physical interaction and location, making the temporal discrepancy more pronounced.

The core contradiction is that Acro claims he couldn't *both* lift the bust *and* look out the window, implying a simultaneous action. The spatial arrangement of the lodging house doesn't directly challenge this claim.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: Acro's lodging house room is above Moe's lodging house room. This is relevant to the vertical positioning but doesn't directly address the contradiction about simultaneous actions.
Prop 6: The Circus Map depicts Acro's lodging house room as being on the third floor. This is relevant to the vertical positioning but doesn't directly address the contradiction about simultaneous actions.
Prop 8: The window in Acro's lodging house room is disconnected from the location of the victim's head. This is the most relevant spatial proposition. It directly supports Acro's claim that he couldn't have seen the victim's head from his window.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 8, "The window in Acro's lodging house room is disconnected from the location of the victim's head," directly supports Acro's testimony. Acro states he couldn't have known the location of the Ringmaster's head. Prop 8 explains *why* – because his window didn't provide a view of the location where the head was found. This contradicts the implication that he *could* have known the location if he had simply looked out the window, which is the core of the contradiction.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

Testimony 1 states, "When I opened the door to the joint, I saw one ugly scene." Testimony 2 continues, "The guy was laid out over the table, stiff as concrete." This implies Tigre saw the victim immediately upon entering Tres Bien. However, Evidence 11 (Tres Bien Floor Plans) and Evidence 12 (Crime Photo) state that the entrance to Tres Bien obstructed the view of the patio and that the victim was found collapsed on a table *near* a coffee table, with papers scattered around. It's unlikely Tigre would have had a clear view of the victim's body immediately upon entering, given the obstructed view and the victim's position relative to the entrance.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 5: The entrance to Tres Bien obstructed the view of the patio.
Prop 1: The victim was in front of the coffee table in Tres Bien.
Prop 2: The folding screen in Tres Bien was between two dining tables.

**Step 3: Propositions revealing the contradiction**

Prop 5 directly contradicts Tigre's testimony. If the entrance obstructed the view of the patio, it would also likely obstruct a clear view of the interior, including the table where the victim was found. Testimony 1 and 2 suggest an immediate, unobstructed view, which is impossible given the spatial layout described in Prop 5 and supported by Evidence 11 and 12. Prop 1 and 2 further reinforce this, as they describe the victim's location relative to other objects within the restaurant, implying a more complex spatial arrangement than Tigre's immediate observation suggests.

**Step 4: N/A**

**Step 5:** {"evidence": 11, "testimony": 1}

**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Evidence 9 (Crime Photo) and Testimony 5 (Gumshoe's claim about the knife). Evidence 9 depicts a crime scene where the victim is sitting on a chair, with a glass of juice behind him and a full bottle of juice on the desk in front of him. Testimony 5 states that the defendant (Engarde) bought the knife *for* the crime, implying premeditation. However, the crime photo shows a full bottle of juice on the desk. If Engarde premeditated the murder, he would likely have removed the juice bottle to avoid leaving a potential piece of evidence. The presence of the full bottle suggests a scenario where the murder was not as planned as Gumshoe claims.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 4: The guitar case is near the victim.
Prop 5: The wine glass is near the victim.
Prop 6: The autograph is near the victim.
Prop 9: The spy camera is inside the victim's room.

These propositions all relate to the spatial arrangement of items near the victim, which is relevant to the contradiction. Prop 9 is particularly important as it establishes the presence of a recording device, potentially capturing events leading up to the murder.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 9 (The spy camera is inside the victim's room) is the most relevant. The existence of a spy camera recording the victim's room from 8 PM onwards directly challenges Gumshoe's assertion of premeditation. If Engarde had planned the murder, he would have likely disabled or removed the camera. The fact that the camera was running suggests the murder occurred unexpectedly or during a situation where Engarde didn't anticipate being recorded. This contradicts the idea of a carefully planned, premeditated attack. The other propositions (4, 5, 6) simply describe the scene, but Prop 9 introduces a temporal element that undermines Gumshoe's narrative.

It suggests a possible alternative scenario where Wocky was not solely relying on the pistol.

Testimonies 2, 3, and 4 establish a front-facing relationship, with the pistol pointed at the victim. Prop 10 suggests the pistol was *behind* Pal Meraktis, which is spatially inconsistent with the witness's account. The presence of the knife (Evidence 10) further complicates the picture, suggesting a different weapon or a different position for Wocky.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The spatial reasoning here isn't about physical location but about *relative position*. The contradiction arises from the conflicting descriptions of the lighter's position in the perpetrator's hand. Aura's statement establishes a spatial relationship (left hand), while the evidence implies a different spatial relationship (right hand, based on fingerprint placement).

**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Evidence 6 (Chip Photo) and Testimony 3 (Olga's testimony about five aces). Evidence 6 shows the chip distribution and the cards in each player's hand. It depicts Wright having 7 of hearts, 7 of diamonds, 7 of clubs, Ace of spades, and Ace of diamonds, and Smith having King of hearts, King of diamonds, King of spades, Ace of clubs, and Ace of hearts. This clearly shows *two* aces in Wright's hand and *one* ace in Smith's hand. Testimony 3 states that Wright has two aces and Smith has three, totaling five aces. This is spatially impossible given the cards shown in the Chip Photo.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 4: The chips belonging to the defendant are to the left of the chips belonging to the victim. - This is a spatial relationship described in the evidence, but doesn't directly reveal the contradiction.
Prop 6: The crime photo 2 shows the victim's forehead, which is below the hat. - This is irrelevant to the contradiction.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 9 is relevant because it establishes the spatial proximity of the hands. However, the contradiction isn't about their proximity, but about the *contents* of those hands. The spatial proximity (Prop 9) highlights the fact that the cards are physically present and observable, making Olga's claim about five aces demonstrably false when compared to the visual evidence (Chip Photo).

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning**

The spatial reasoning is based on the visual representation of the cards in Evidence 6. The Chip Photo provides a concrete, spatial depiction of the cards held by each player. Olga's testimony, claiming five aces, contradicts this visual evidence. The contradiction isn't about the location of objects, but about the *quantity* of a specific object (aces) as depicted spatially in the Chip Photo versus Olga's verbal claim.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 4 ("I saw bullet holes in the wall, and knew Mr. LeTouse had been shot... Then I was led by the police to Mr. Gavin's dressing room, and told to wait there.") and Evidence 10 (Diagram: "Observing from the door, the victim was blocked from sight by items."). Lamiroir claims to have seen the bullet holes *after* seeing LeTouse's body behind a wall. However, the diagram indicates that items blocked the view from the entrance door, making it impossible to see both the victim and the bullet holes simultaneously from that position. If items were blocking the view, she couldn't have seen the bullet holes unless she moved from the entrance door.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: Lamiroir was in the entrance door and the backstage was behind her - This aligns with Lamiroir's testimony of being at the entrance door.
Prop 2: The victim, LeTouse, was behind a wall relative to Lamiroir's position at the entrance door - This also aligns with Lamiroir's testimony.
Prop 3: The bullet holes in the wall were in front of Lamiroir when she was at the entrance door - This directly contradicts the diagram (Evidence 10) which states items blocked the view from the entrance door.
Prop 4: Lamiroir was in Mr. Gavin's dressing room after she saw the bullet holes - This is a consequence of her testimony, but not the core contradiction.

**Step 3: Propositions revealing the contradiction**

Prop 3 directly reveals the contradiction. It states the bullet holes were *in front* of Lamiroir at the entrance door. However, Evidence 10 (Diagram) states items *blocked* her view from the entrance door. These two statements are mutually exclusive; if items blocked her view, the bullet holes couldn't have been in front of her. This implies Lamiroir either moved from the entrance door or is not being truthful about what she saw.

**Step 4: Spatial reasoning**

The core spatial reasoning is about line of sight. The diagram establishes a blocked view from the entrance door. Lamiroir's testimony implies a clear view of both the victim and the bullet holes from that same location. This is spatially impossible given the diagram's depiction of the scene.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 1 ("That's when I saw Juniper dragging a large mat from behind the stage.") and Testimony 3 ("She was heading towards me, so I assume she was on her way to the maintenance area."). This implies Juniper was moving *from* behind the stage *towards* the vantage point (where O'Conner was). However, Testimony 4 ("But I didn't have time to stand around and see what she did after that.") introduces uncertainty about Juniper's subsequent actions. The core contradiction is the assumption of Juniper's destination (the maintenance area) based on a limited observation.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Several spatial propositions are relevant:

*   **Prop 1:** The stage is to the left of the maintenance area.
*   **Prop 2:** The vantage point is between the stage and the maintenance area.
*   **Prop 4:** The mat was behind the stage.
*   **Prop 8:** The construction screen is between the stage and the vantage point.
*   **Prop 10:** The person watching the stage was near the vantage point.

**Step 3: Propositions revealing the contradiction**

Props 1, 2, and 10 are most relevant. Prop 2 states the vantage point is *between* the stage and the maintenance area. Testimony 3 assumes Juniper was heading *towards* the maintenance area. If the vantage point is between the stage and the maintenance area, and O'Conner (the person watching the stage) was near the vantage point (Prop 10), then Juniper moving "towards" O'Conner would mean she was moving *away* from the maintenance area, contradicting O'Conner's assumption. Prop 4 confirms the mat's initial location "behind the stage," further solidifying the spatial context.

**Step 4: Spatial Reasoning**

The contradiction arises from a misinterpretation of direction. O'Conner assumes "towards me" (the vantage point) equates to "towards the maintenance area." However, given the spatial arrangement (stage, vantage point, maintenance area), these are not necessarily the same direction. The limited observation and subsequent assumption create the spatial inconsistency.

------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 4 (L'Belle's statement about fleeing down the hallway to the right) and Prop 1 (the phone is to the right of the foyer). L'Belle states he fled "down the hallway to his right." This implies the hallway he fled *from* was the foyer. However, Prop 1 states the phone is to the *right of the foyer*. If L'Belle fled *from* the foyer, and the phone is to the right of the foyer, then the hallway he fled down *must* contain the phone. Testimony 3 states the demon emerged from its prison. Testimony 4 states L'Belle fled down the hallway to his right. This implies the demon emerged from the prison and L'Belle fled down the hallway to his right. This creates a spatial contradiction.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: The phone at the end of the hallway is to the right of the foyer according to the Crime Scene Diagram.
Prop 4: The statue depicting Tenma Taro and the Nine-Tailed Fox was at the crime scene.
Prop 5: The Forbidden Chamber is not in the same room as the foyer.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Prop 1 directly applies. L'Belle's testimony places him fleeing "down the hallway to his right" *from* the foyer. Prop 1 establishes the phone is to the right of the foyer. This means the hallway L'Belle fled down *must* contain the phone. However, the testimonies do not mention the phone. The testimonies only mention the demon and L'Belle fleeing. This creates a spatial inconsistency.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning**

The spatial reasoning is based on the relative positions described in Prop 1 and the direction L'Belle claims to have fled. If the foyer is the starting point, and the phone is to its right, then any hallway leading to the right from the foyer would logically contain the phone. The lack of mention of the phone in the testimonies, combined with L'Belle's directional claim, creates a spatial contradiction.

The spatial context of the poker game (Evidence 2) becomes suspicious if the entire scenario was a failed setup.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

*   **Prop 1:** The bottle used as the murder weapon was on the table in the sub-basement. (Supports Testimony 3, but also links the weapon to the scene of the failed setup)

*   **Prop 3:** Olga was in the sub-basement during the time of the crime. (Supports Olga's testimony and places her at the scene of both the failed trap and the alleged murder)
*   **Prop 4:** The cards and chips were on the table in the sub-basement. (Reinforces the poker game setting described in Evidence 2)
*   **Prop 5:** The small red chips were near the defendant Wright in the sub-basement. (Links Wright to the table and the poker game)

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

Propositions 1, 2, 3, 4, and 5 all contribute to the spatial context of the crime. The contradiction arises because the poker game setting (supported by Props 1, 2, 4, and 5) seems inconsistent with the sudden, impulsive act of violence described in Testimony 3 and 4. If Olga's testimony about the failed trap is true, the poker game might have been staged, and Wright's actions might not have been as spontaneous as Olga claims. The presence of the bottle on the table (Prop 1) becomes more suspicious in this context.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

The spatial reasoning focuses on the location of the crime (sub-basement) and the objects present (cards, chips, bottle). The contradiction isn't about the *absolute* location, but about the *relationship* between the location, the objects, and the events described in the testimonies. The poker game setting creates a specific spatial narrative that clashes with the narrative of a sudden, unplanned attack.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Testimony 3 ("The doctor was standing in front of the bookshelves") and Evidence 3 ("The victim is lying in the study next to floor-to-ceiling windows with clear glass. He is covered by books."). Testimony 3 states the doctor was *standing* in front of the bookshelves, implying an upright position. Evidence 3 states the victim is *lying* covered by books, suggesting a prone position near the windows. These two statements cannot both be true simultaneously; a person cannot be both standing and lying down at the same time.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 1: The victim was below the bookcase in the study - This aligns with Evidence 3, but contradicts Testimony 3.
Prop 6: The victim was in front of the bookshelf - This aligns with Testimony 3, but contradicts Evidence 3.

**Step 3: Propositions revealing the contradiction**

Both Prop 1 and Prop 6 highlight the contradiction. Prop 6 directly supports Atishon's testimony that the doctor was in front of the bookshelf. However, Evidence 3 describes the victim lying down *next to* the window and covered in books, which is incompatible with being "in front of" the bookshelf in an upright position. Prop 1 further emphasizes the spatial discrepancy by placing the victim *below* the bookcase, reinforcing the idea that the victim was lying down.

**Step 4: Spatial Reasoning**

The core spatial reasoning is the incompatibility of standing and lying positions. Testimony 3 describes a standing position, while Evidence 3 describes a lying position. These are mutually exclusive spatial states.

This creates a spatial contradiction: if the meeting room is the crime scene, why is a suspect in the storeroom?

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Several spatial propositions are relevant:

*   **Prop 1:** The P.I.C. meeting room is above the storeroom. This establishes a vertical relationship between the two locations.
*   **Prop 2:** The storeroom is accessible via a hidden elevator from the P.I.C. meeting room. This establishes a connection between the two locations, allowing movement between them.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Props 1 and 2 are crucial. Prop 1 establishes that the storeroom is *below* the P.I.C. meeting room. Prop 2 explains *how* someone could move between them. Testimony 3 insists the crime occurred *solely* in the meeting room. However, Evidence 17 shows someone in the storeroom. The elevator (Prop 2) provides a plausible route for someone to move between the two locations, suggesting the crime might have involved both spaces, contradicting Franziska's assertion that the meeting room was the *only* crime scene.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain**

Not applicable, as Props 1 and 2 directly illuminate the spatial contradiction.

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Evidence 6 ("Photo of Victim") and Testimony 2 ("The defendant was spotted then, standing in front of the body holding the murder weapon."). Evidence 6 states the victim was "face up in the broken lantern." Testimony 2 states the defendant (presumably Ellen) was "standing in front of the body holding the murder weapon." If the victim is face up *in* the lantern, it would be difficult, if not impossible, for someone to be standing *in front* of the body *inside* the lantern. The spatial relationship described by the two doesn't align.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 3: "The Time Keeper was in front of the victim and the victim was face up in the broken lantern" - This proposition supports the testimony, stating the Time Keeper (presumably held by the defendant) was in front of the victim. However, it doesn't address the spatial relationship between the defendant and the victim *within* the lantern.

Prop 1: "The victim's body was inside the broken lantern and the broken lantern was in the reception hall" - This proposition confirms the victim's location within the lantern.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction**

Prop 1 and Prop 3, when considered together, highlight the contradiction. Prop 1 establishes the victim is *inside* the lantern. Prop 3 places the Time Keeper (and by implication, the person holding it) *in front of* the victim. This creates a spatial conflict: if the victim is contained within the lantern, the person holding the weapon cannot simultaneously be standing *in front* of the victim *inside* the lantern.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction**

The contradiction is identified through basic spatial reasoning. The phrase "face up in" implies containment. "Standing in front of" implies an external position. These two positions are mutually exclusive within the described spatial context.

## OBJECTIVE:
**FIND THE CONTRADICTION:** Which evidence and testimony contradict each other?

## CONSTRAINT:
**You must only answer ONE PAIR.**

## ANALYSIS FRAMEWORK:
Please follow this format:

**Step 1:** **Analyze the spatial contradiction** between evidences and testimonies

**Step 2:** **Check if any spatial propositions** from the list above apply to this contradiction

**Step 3:** **If applicable propositions exist,** explicitly state which proposition(s) and explain how they reveal the contradiction

**Step 4:** **If none of the listed propositions apply,** explain the spatial reasoning used to identify the contradiction

**Step 5:** **Provide your answer in JSON format**

## CRITICAL OUTPUT REQUIREMENT:
**Your answer must end with a JSON format like so:** {"evidence": 2, "testimony": 3}

**The answer JSON should strictly be the last line, with nothing before or after it.**

LLM OUTPUT (RAW RESPONSE):
------------------------------
**Step 1: Analyze the spatial contradiction**

The contradiction lies between Evidence 11 (Det. Gumshoe's Fingerprints on the hallway bench, with a sweet sugar scent) and Testimony 2 (Gumshoe claims to have been in the hallway the whole time until he heard the gunshot). Testimony 3 reinforces this, stating he didn't take a single step away from the Lobby No. 2 door. However, Evidence 11 indicates he sat on the bench and ate a Swiss roll, implying a degree of relaxation and movement within the hallway, which contradicts the rigid adherence to the Lobby No. 2 door described in his testimony. While being *in* the hallway is consistent, the act of sitting and eating suggests a more casual presence than the testimony implies.

**Step 2: Check if any spatial propositions apply**

Prop 2: The hallway bench is in the hallway where Gumshoe was stationed. This proposition confirms the spatial relationship between the bench and Gumshoe's location.
Prop 1: Gumshoe was in the hallway and therefore near Lobby No. 2 during his guard duty. This supports Gumshoe's testimony about being in the hallway.
Prop 4: The pink balloon was on the windowsill of the hallway window where Gumshoe was stationed. This places Gumshoe in the same general area.

**Step 3: If applicable propositions exist, state which and explain**

Propositions 1 and 2 support Gumshoe's general location. However, the combination of Prop 2 (bench in the hallway) and Evidence 11 (fingerprints on the bench) creates a contradiction with Testimony 2 and 3. The testimonies suggest Gumshoe was essentially glued to the Lobby No. 2 door, while the evidence suggests he was relaxed enough to sit and eat a Swiss roll on a bench within the hallway. This implies a degree of movement and a less rigid adherence to his post than he claims.

**Step 4: If none of the listed propositions apply, explain**

Not applicable, as Propositions 1 and 2 directly relate to the spatial contradiction.

{"evidence": 11, "testimony": 3}
